New Delhi, the 17th Fehiuaty, 1990 

SO 408.—Whereas in exercise of the powers confetred 
hv Section 6 of the Export (Quality Control & Inspection) 
Act, 1963 (22 of 1963), the Central Government is of /he 
opinion that it is necessary and eN>cdi®nt so to do for toe 
development of the Export Trade of India that Basmati Rice 
should be subject to qnehtv control and inspection prior 
to exDort t 


And whereas the Central Government has formulated the 
proposals specified below for the sail'purpose and has for¬ 
warded the same to the Export Inspection Council as required 
by sub-rule (2)-of rule 11 of the Export (Quality Control and 
Inspection) Rules, 1964, 

Now, therefore, in pursuance of the said sub-mlc the Cen¬ 
tral Government, in supersession of the notification of the 
Goveinmcnt of India in the Ministry of Commerce S.O. No. 
I(i25 and 1026 dated 19th April, 1980 as •^®tdcd by S.a 
824(E) dated 30th September, 1980 and S.O. 3916 dated 
toth October, 1983 relating to Basmati Rice, hereby publishes 
the said proposals for the information of the public liKcIy to 

hi* nflVi'tf.rf tnercfhv. 


2 Notice is hereby given that any person desirmg to for¬ 
ward any objections or suggestions with respect to the said 
proposals may forward the same within forwflye days from 
the date of publication of this order in the Oircial Gaz^te to 
the Export Inspection Council, Pragati Tower, 11th Floor, 
26, Rujendra Place, New Dclhi-110008, 


PROPOSALS 

(1) To notify that Basmati Rice shall be subject to quality 
control and inspection prior to export. 

(2) To specify the type of quality control and inspection 
in accordance with the draft of the proposed E.xport of Bas¬ 
mati Rice (In.spection) Rules, 1990 us set out m the Annexure 
appended to this Order, as the type of quality control and 
inspection which shall be applied to such Basmati Rice prior 
to their export; 

(3) To recognise— 

(a) The national standards of importing countries and 
international standards; 


272 G1190—2 



474 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA ; FEBRUARY 


(b) Tho contractual specifications agreed to between the 

foreign buyer and the exporter provided that speci¬ 
fications are not below the minimum specifleationa 
stipulated in the Schedules J to 4 appended to this 
Order. 

(c) The grade designation,. formulated under Basmati 

Rice (Fxport) Grading and Marking Rules, 1980; 

Provided that such specifications are in conformity 
ii^ith clause (b); 

(d) In the absence of contractual specifications the mini¬ 

mum specifications set out in the Sclredule to the 
Order ; ■ 

Provided that the specifications under clause (a), 

(b), (c) and (d) shall also conform to the Food 
Laws if any, inforce in the importing country. 


17, U)90/MAGHA lift, 1911 [Part II-Sf,c. 3(ii)] 


(.4) To prohibit the export, in the course of international 
trade of Basniati Rice unless a mark or seal recognised by 
the Central Government indicating that it conforms to the 
standard specifications applicable to it hag been affi.xed or ap- 
plicri to packages or containers of such Bnsmati Rice and 
is accompanied by a certificate of Grade/lnspection issued 
by the Agricultural Marketing Adviser to the Government 
of India or by any of the agencies established under Section 
7 of the Export (Quality Control and Inspection) Act, 19611 
(22 of 1963) to the effect that such Basmati Rice conforms 
to the standard specifications and is exportworthy. 

3. Nothing in this Order shall apply to export by sea, land 
or air of samples of Basmati Rice not exceeding in value 
of rupees fifty to prospective buyers, ' 

4. In this Order, “Basmati Rice (Oryza Saliva)” shall mean 
Basmati Raw Milled Rice, Parboiled Rice and Dehusfced and 
Unpolished (Brown) Basmati Rice produced in India.

(2) Tho rice : 


(a) shall be dried matured kernels of Oryza Sativa; and have uniform size, shape and colour; 

fb) shall possess in a marked degree the natural fragrance characteristic of Basmati Rice both in (he raw and cooked state; 

(c) shall not have been artificially coloured and shall be free from polishing agents; 

(d) rOay contain upto 3 percent of grains with an appreciable amount of bran thereon; 

(e) shall be free ftom musty obnoxious odour and shall carry no sign of mould or containing webs and dead or live weevils; 

(f) shall have length 6.0 mm and above and length breadth ratio 3 and above; 

(g) shall be in sound merchantable condition,

Definitions 

(i) Foreign Matter : shall include dust, stones, lumps or earth, chaff, steams or straw and any other Impurity. 

(ii) Brokens and fragments ; shall Include pieces of rice kernels which arc less than three-fourth of a whole kernel. The pieces of 
kernels, smaller than one-fourth of the whole kernels shall be treated as fragments. 

(iil) Other rice Including rod grains ; shall consist of contransing and/or Inferior varieties of rice. Red grains shall be tho kei nels, 
whole or broken which have 25 percent or more of their surface coated with red bran, 

(iv) Damaged, discoloured and chalky grains : shall include rice kenrcls -brokens, fragments of whole that arc intonernally damaled 
or discoloured materially affecting the quality. Chalky grains shall be the grains atleast half of which is milky white in colour

ANNEXURE 

Draft rules proposed to be made amder Section 17 of the 
Export (Quality Control & Inspection) Act, 1963 (22 of 1963) 
in supersession of the notification No. S.O. 1626 dated 19th 
April, 1980, except in respect of things done or omitted to 
be done before such supersession. 

1. Short title and commencement—(1) These rules may 
be called the Export of Basmati Rice (Quality Control imd 
Inspection) Rules, 1990; 

(2) They shall come into force on the date of their publi¬ 
cation in the Official GaMtte. 

2. Definitions.—In these rules, unless the context otherwise 
requires :— ' 


tral Governmern^rSn^nf 

(a) By ensuring that the product has been processed hv 


inspection in accordance with rule 3, S cy to curry out 


(a) ‘Act’ means the Export (Quality Control and Ins¬ 
pection) Act, 1963 (22 of 1963); 

(b) ‘Agency’" means any of the Export Inspection .'\gen- 
cies established by the Central Government at 
Bombay,. Calcutta, Cochin, Delhi and Madras under 
Section 7 of the Act for inspection or the Agricul¬ 
tural Marking Adviser to the Government of India 
or any other officer authorised on his behalf for 
inspection; 

(c) ‘Council’ means the Export Inspection Council estab¬ 

lished under Section 3 of the Act; 

(d) "Basmati Rice (Oryzii Sativa)’’ shall mean Basmati 
Raw Milled Rice, Rarboiled Rice and Dehusked and 
Unpolished (Brown) Basmati Rice produced in 
India. 

3. Basis of Inspection.—The inspection of Basmati Rice 
shall be carried out with a view to seeing that the same con- 


Basmati Rice processed by exercising 
en quality control as preseribed by the S- 

Tifcu ® P^n’POse, the exportcj- shall also fur¬ 

nish alongw.th the intimation mentioned in sub-rule fl) n 

Son C bee " Basmati Rke iffiended for 

been proceiised by the exercising udeciLiatc aualitv 
By the council and the coSmenTcom 
foims to the standard specification recognised for the piir- 


les! under sub-rule (1) shall be given not 

Jess than 2 days before the inspection to be c.anicd out at 
the premrses situated at the same station where the office of 
(he Agency is located and not less (han 10 days before the 
he/‘tnied out at the premises which are nit 
situated at the same station where the office of ih- Agenev 
IS localed. ^ 




['fn<r 3 (li)] 


477 


=fT:'FTTsfV i7, lago/’rn' 3 8, i9ii 


(4) On receipt of the intimation lutclcr sub-rule (1) and the 
declaration if any, under sub-rule (2), the Agency-- 

(a) On satisfying itself that the processor has exercised 

adequate quality control as prescribed by the Coun¬ 
cil in this regard to ensure that the product con¬ 
form to the standard specification recognised for the 
purpose, shall within 2 days issue a eertifleate de¬ 
claring the consignment of Bnsmali Rice as export- 
worthy. 

(b) Ill case where the processor is not the exporter, the 
consignment shall bo physically verified and such veri¬ 
fication and/or inspection, if any necessary, shall be 
carried out by the Agency to ensure that the above 
conditions aro complied with. 

(5) (a) The Agency shall, however, carry out the spot 
check of some of the consignments meant for export and also 
visit the processing units at regular intervals to verify the 
adequacy of Inprocess quality control drill adopted by the 
unit. If the processing unit is found not mainiaining the re¬ 
quired quality control measures at any stage of the processing 
or does not comply with the recommendations of the Coun¬ 
cil/Agency, the unit shall be declared as not having adei^uate 
in process quality control drills. In such cases, the unit, if so 
desires, shall apply afresh for adjudgement of the mainte¬ 
nance of adequacy of inprocess quality control drills. 

(b) In case where the exporter has not declared under sub- 
rule (2) that adequate quality control as laid down by the 
Council had boon exercised, the Agency on .satisfying itself 
that the consignment of Basmati Rice conforms to the stand¬ 
ard specification recognised for the purpose on the basis of 
inspection and testing carried out as laid down by the Coun¬ 
cil, shall within 2 or 10 days as the case may be, issue a certi¬ 
ficate declaring the consignment of Basmati Rice as export¬ 
worthy ; 

Provided that where the Agency is not satisfied, it shall 
refuse to issue a certificate to the exporter declaring the con¬ 
signment of Basmati Rice as exportworthy and shall com¬ 
municate such refusal within 2 or 10 days, as the case may 
be, to the exporter alongwith the reasons thereof, 

(6) Subsequent to certification, the Agency .shall have the 
right to reassess the quality of the consignment at any place 
or storage, in transit or at the ports before its actual ship¬ 
ment ; 

(7) In the event of Ihe consignment being found not con¬ 
forming to the standard specifications at any of these stages, 
the certificate of inspection originally issued shall be with¬ 
drawn. 

5. Packing and Marking.—-An e.xportcr intending to pack 
Basmati Rice for export after preparing the consignment as 
per the rule .shall pack in standard new gunny bags/canvas 
bags with inner polythcno/cardboard cartons multilayer poly- 
laminates or as per specific requirements of the buyer. 

The following information shall be stencilled/printed on 
bags/packages: 

1. Name and address of the exporter; 

2. Variety ; 

3. Grade ; 

4. Lot Number ; 

5. Gross weight and net weight; 

6 . Product of India ; 

7. Shipment Mark. 

6 . Place of Inspection.—^Inspection for the purpose of these 
rules shall be carried out at the premises of the exporter 
where the goods are offered for inspection, provided that 
adequate facilities e.xist therein for inspection. 

7. Inspection Fee.—A lee at the rates prescribed I'or the 
purpose of inspection shall be paid to the Agency as Inspec¬ 
tion fee for consignmentwise inspection and inprocess quality 
control respectively. 


8. Apjteal: 

(a) Aliy exporter aggrieved by the refusal of the Agency 
to issue the certificate of inspection within 10 days 
of such refusal prefer an appeal which shall be 
referred by the Agency to a Panel of Exports con¬ 
stituted for the purpose ; 

fb; The quorum of the Panel shall be three ; 

(c) The appeal shall be disposed of within fifteen days 

from its receipt; 

(d) The decision of the Panel in such nppead shall be 
final.

S.O. 413 ;—Whereas by the notification of the Govemmeot of India in the Ministry of Energy (.Department of Coal) No. 8,0.12(KE) 
dated the 25lh February, 1987 under sub-section li) of Section 7 of the Coal Bearing Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 
(20 of 1957), and publisned in the Gazette of India, Exlra-ordinary, Part-II, Section 3, Sub-section (ii) dated the 25th February, 1987 the 
Central Government gave notice of its Intention to acquire land and rights in the locality specified in the Schedule append^ to that 
notification. 

And whereas the competent authority in pursuance of section 8 of the said Act has made his leport to the Central Government; 

And whereas the Central Government after considering the report aforesaid and after consulting the Government of Maharashtra 
is satisfied that the lands nteasuring 1132.30 hectares (approximately) or 2796.78 acres (approximately) described in the schedule 
appended hereto should be acquired. 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (i) of Section 9 of the said Act, the Central Govermnent hereby 
declares that the lands measuring 1132.30 hectare (approximately) or 2776.78 acres (apiiroximatcly) described In the said &hedule are 
hereby acquired, “ - 

The plan No. C-l(E)niyjJU,'424-0988 dated 21st September, 1988 of the area covered by this notificaiion may be Inspected m the 
office of the Collector , Yavatmal (Maharashtra) or in the office of the Coal Controller, 1, Council House Street, Calcutta or in the Office 
of the Western Coalfields Limited (Revenue Department), Coal Estate, Civil Lines, Nagpur (Muharaslnrai.

The Central Guveinment has Tcfcrred the following In¬ 
dustrial Dispute between the M|s, S.G.S. India Private 
l.imitcd, Vasco-da-Gama and their workmen, to this Tri¬ 
bunal under Section 10(1 )fdj of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 : 

"Whether the action of the maiiagomcnt of M|s. S.G.S. 
(India) Pvt. 1 td, Vasco-da-Gama in terminating 
the services of Sh. Solon Fernandes, Casual Sam¬ 
pler' w.e.f. January, 1987 and not considering him 
for regular cmpicvymcnt is justified? If not, what 
relief the said workman is entitled to ?” 

2. The reference was received here in November 1989. 
'I hereafter, the matter was fixed for the statement of claim 
of the workman and the written Statement of the Manage¬ 
ment on as many as three dates, that is to say 7th and 
22nd December, 1989, and 16-1-1990. Notice for ca£h of 
these three dales is duly served on the workmen. However, 
they do not appear to be interested in submitting their state¬ 
ment of claim by appearing before the Tribunal or by post. 
Consequently, the management have also not filed their 
written statement. I do not see that any useful purpose will 
he served by sending any further notice to the Union of 
the workmen. 

3. In absence of any evidence it is not po,ssible to hold 
th.it ilie action of the Management is not jnstifled. I accord- 
in.fily hold that Iheir action is not shown to he unjustified 
and as such the workman is not entitled to any relief. 
Award accoiclingly,

AWARD 

This industrial dispute between State Bank Of SaUrashffaj

D.IKAl dated 21st August, 1982. 

2. The dispute relates to a single demand of the workmen 
which is as under :— 

“WTiether the action of the management of State Bank 
of Saurashtra in terminating the service of Shri 
Vijubhai Rayabhai Chekhalia, Godown Chowkidar 
Jawahar Chowk Branch. Surendranagar W'ith effect 
from i-8-79 is justified? If not. to what relief is 
the workman concerned entitled?” 

3. The workman concerned has filed statement of claim 
at Ex. 2 contending inter alia that he was appointed as 
sub-staff in the services of the State Bank of Saurashtra 
(hereinafter referred to as ‘the Bank’) in the month of 
March, 1979 and he resumed his duties on 5th March, 
1979, He then continued to work as sub-staff and his ser¬ 
vices were then arbitrarily and abriipdy terminating on 
31st July, 1979 afternoon that during the tenure of his 
service his conduct W'as 'very good. He was not given 
any show cause notice or there was no 
reason for complaint against him; that he was requesting 
the management for confirmation in the service of the 
Bank as he had completed three month’s service in the 
Bank as per the Awards and Settlements in vogue; that he 
was appointed on permanent clear vacancy; that the Bank 
got annoyed at his request and then terminated his services 
as stated above; that the action taken against him is puni¬ 
tive and he has been victimised with ulterior motive. He 
has, therefore, requested that he may be reinstated on his 
original post of sub-staff with full back wages and continuiiy 
of service and aNo the fringe benefits which were available 
to him. 

4. On behalf of the Bank the written statement has been 
filed at Ex. 5 wherein the Bank has contended that the work¬ 
man concerned having been appointed as a temporary 
workman and as the temporary work was over, his services 
had come to an end legally and poperly. It was also stated 
that the workman concerned had not come with clean hands. 
He had suppressed certain materials facts regarding his 
qualifications while seeking temporary appointment. His 
other allegation this his services were terminated because 
he bad requested for his confirmation was denied; that it 
v,as also denied that he was entitled to be made permanent 
under the provisions of the Awards and Settlements as 
alleged bv him. The Bank also denied that the services of 
the workman concerned were illegally and improperly ter- 
m’na'ed. According to the Bank, the true facts are that 
the workman concerned was employed purely in the tem¬ 
porary canacity as godown chowk'dar in the godown of 
one of the borrowers of the Bank for the period from 
5-3-1979 to 31-7-1979. He used to be given appointment 
letters fiom time to time for his temnorary anooinfment 
and on exniiv of h's contractual period he was remtsved 
from his temnorarv assignment. He had also not comnleted 
240 davs service in the Bank in a calendar year. It was 
also stated that the workman concerned wh'iho annlvir.g fr.r 
the temnorarv nost made an annlication dated 5-.3-1979 
.and mentioned i-is educational oualifications as Standard VTf. 
However, the Bank subsequently learnt that the workman 
concerned had nassed F.Y.B. Cora, examination in the yoar 
1978; that as far the rules of the Bank are concerned, 
only nnn-m.afriajl.'itr can be cmploved in the said temporary 


jbb and also in the subordinate cadre; that the appiicant 
aeiiHeratdv stated in his application the wrong qualification 
and also did not pioduCe the necessary certificate. 'Ihus 
the applicant h,.d i^iislcd the Bank by suppressn-g the 
material fact about his higher qualifications and secured 
fraudulently the temporary employment with tiie _ Bank: 
that the workman concerned was not eligible for appointment 
In the post as per the rules and regulations of the Bank. In 
view of the above, it was submiUed that the demand deserves 
to be rejected.

7. As against that the Bank has examined one Praful- 
chandra Shah at bx. 16. He says that he is working as a 
Manager and he is in the service of the Bank for the last 
26 years. When the workman was given the aboVfc.said ap¬ 
pointment, he was working in that branch and it is he who 
had given him the appointment letter. After jersuing the 
exhibits of his appointment letters he clearly staled that he 
was given temporary appointment as a chowkidar as at the 
relevant lime the necessity had arisen. He also slated that 
after that period was over, he was discharged because Ihere- 
alter there was no work. He also stated that in the post of 
, temporary chawkidars and temporary peons candidates 
below the age of 24 and those w'lio have not passed S.S.C. 
aie given the said post. According to him this is accord¬ 
ing to the office circular in that behalt. When shown the 
application made by the workmen concerned, after seeing 
the same he had stated that in the said application the 
workman concerned had mentioned his qualification as VII 
Vernacular passed and after considering tlie facts mentioned 
there in the said application he w'as given temporary 
appointment. Thereafter the management came to know 
that the workman concerned had already passed S.S.C. on 
the date ol application. He then stated that because he 
was purely temporary and as there was no work for him 
he was discharged. The W'itness has been cross-examined 
at length and the witness has stated there in that theie was a 
temporary post of a chowkidar at one of the factories viz. 
Laxmi Potteries; that the workmen concerned w'as given 
.appoin*ment in the subordinate staff. Then he once again 
made it clear that appointment in the subordinate staff is 
given only to these who are not S.S.C. and made it further 
clear that no appointment can be given to those who 
passed S,S.C. He at the same time denied very categoric¬ 
ally thta it is not because of this reason viz. that the work¬ 
man concerned had chea'ed the Bank that he has been 
discharged. He also denied to have taken the work of 
Peon from the workman concerned though he was appointed 
as a temporary watchman for Laxmi Potteries. The Bank 
also examined one Bhagatiprased Dolatram at Ex. 19 who 
in his deposition states that he knows the workman con¬ 
cerned as he was also working in the same branch where 
the workman concerned was working. He further slated 
that the workman concerned was taken up as a temporary 
chowkidar but then as it was found that thereafter his 
.services were not required and as he was temporary w'ateh- 
m.m he was discharged. He also stated clearly that he was 
not. discharged for any misconduct. He then stated that 
after Ive was discharged as he was temporary chowkidar, 
the fact about his qualification had come to the notice of 
the Bank. He then made it clear that new there is no ques¬ 
tion of giving him further appointment as he had already 
passed S.S.C. or even B. Com. This witness was also cross- 
exanr'ned at length but here also the wi'ness has stated very 
c'early that the workman concerned was given temporary 
appo'ntment. The witness has however, admitted that the 
fartoiy on which the workman concerned was given ap- 
pi'ntinent was closed when he was taken up and it wa”. 
al'o elos'ed when he was discharged; that after looking 
at Lx 20 he had stated that the workman concerned was 
taken up as as a godown chakidar as mentioned therein. 
He, however, admitted jthat the workman concerned was 
not given any notice or notice pay hut mentioned that he 
was discharged as there was no work for him and because 
he was a temporary hand. 


S’, Shri T, R. Mishra had apoeared for the workman 
corii.erned and Shii Maheshbhai T. Parikh for the Bank. 


0 Shii Mishra in support of the case of the workman 
concerned pointed out from various appointment letters 
that the workman concerned had worked for 5 different 
'p.e'Is Firstlv for 27 davs, then 30 davs, thereafter 31 
days, then 30 davs and lastly for 31 day.s. Thus in all he 
work’d for C9 days This clearlv shows that he had not 
compeied 240 d.iys in one calend.rr year. Shri Mishra then 
araned that the workman concerned had been discharged 
onlv because it cnyie to the notice of the Bank that the 
workman concerned had suppressed material fact about his 
passing F Y.B Com. examination and that Is the reason 
272 GT/93—6 


why his services have been teiminated. To support this he 
also pointed out from the written statement wherein the 
Bank had stated that the workman concerned had supplied 
wrong materials while enteiing into service. Shri Mishra 
then argued that if the Bank had terminated the services 
of the workman concerned for .supplying wrong information, 
it becomes misconduct and, theretore, an inquiry as has to 
be held which admittedly the Bank had nut done. More¬ 
over, such a misconduct has not been prescribed in the 
Standing Orders and as the Awards governing these emp¬ 
loyees, it is also not a misconduct. It was, therefore, argued 
by Shri Mishra that the termination in the instant case is 

bad in law, Shri Mishra also lelied on the decision render¬ 

ed by the Hon'ble Supreme Court wherein. Their Lord- 
ships have observed “One should lift the veil and find out 
what is what" Shri Mishra also relied on Tila Maz- 
doors case and argued that higher qualifications is not 
a disqiia'ification. Shri Mishra then lastly argued that if a 
workman concerned was a temporal y employee as per the 
Sa.stry Award his services cannot be terminated after com¬ 
pletion of three months. The Bank has also not proved that 
he was a badli workman inasmuch as it has not been shown 
in whose place he had worked. He also argued that as per 
paraniarh 522(4) of the Sastry Award the Bank was re¬ 
quired to give 14 days’ notice before terminating his ser¬ 

vices. 

10. As against that Shri M. T. Parikh for the Bank 
pointed out from the various exhibits that the workman 
concerned was employed only for specific periods by differ¬ 
ent appointment oidcrs and for different spells. U vms also 
pointed out from Ex. 8)9' which is the original application 
given by the workman concerned wherein he had mentioned 
his qualification as Standard VII Vernacular passed. Shri 
Parikh also pointed out from Ex, 7|1 that there are certain 
guidelines for appointment of Peons and Chawkidars. Shri 
Parikh then maintained firmly that the workman concerned 
was discharged because the period for which he was ap¬ 
pointed was over and also pointed out that the Bank cairtfe 
to know about the wrong qualifications mentioned by the 
workman concerned only after some time and also con¬ 
tended that the workman concerned had been discharged 
for any misconduct on his part. He. therefore, argued that 
the action of the Bank is quite leg.il and proper and the re¬ 
ference deserves to be dismissed. 

11. I have considered the conten'-ions of both the sides 
and there are certain facts which are very pertinent in this 
case. It is a fact that the workman concerned had applied 
in the Bank to get the job as a temporary godown chakfdar. 
There was a practice in Bank at the relevant time to give 
appointment as temporary chawkidars for the factories or 
godown of their clients who have borrowed money from 
the Bank, It has also been proved beyond doubt that the 
workman concerned in his application dated 5-3 1979 for 
securing iob had clearly menlioned in his educational quali¬ 
fications Standard VII vernacular passed. Now in his oral 
evidence the workman had in very clear terms admitled that 
the date on which he gave this information, he had already 
passed the F.Y.B. Com. examination. It also appears from 
the oral evidence of the Bank Manager that this info'matton 
came to the knowledge of the Bank after quite a long 
time and this is not the real reason for which the work¬ 
man concerned was discharged but because the period for 
which he was appointed was over and as it was purely a 
temporary job .and as there was no work he was discharg¬ 
ed. Various appointment letters which have been exhibit¬ 
ed also show that the workman concerned was appointed 
for few days viz. for five different spells and for different 
number of days. He has not completed 240 days in the 
calendar vear rnd in my opinion the provisions of Section 
25F would not be attracted herein. As regards the con¬ 
tention of Shri Mishra that as per the Sastnv Award Para 
.522f4) the workman should have been given 14 days’ 
notice i-fi mv opinion this would not apply In the instant 
case inasmuch very recently the similar arguments were 
advanced in the case of this very Bunk wherein it was 
argued on behalf of the Bank that Sastry Award does 
not apply to the Bank viz. the State Bank of, Sanrashtra 
«s the name of the Pank does not appear in Schedule I to 
the Notification d-ated 5-1-1952 issued by the Central 



506 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 17, lyOO/MAGHA ZB, 1011 [Eari U-Sec. 3(ii)] 


Government in this behalf. From the said schedule, it 
docs appear that the name ot the Bank is not included 
therein. As ajainst that it has also not been shown that 
the said award would apply to this Bank or to the work¬ 
men working in the Bank, In the case where such an 
arguments u'ere advanced, T had held that the contentions 
raised on behalf of the Bunk appear to be valid. Jn this 
case also the contention being the same and also the Bank 
being the samc^ I am of the view that as far as the applica¬ 
tion of the Sastry Award ig concerned, the same would 
not be applicuble to this Bank. The question, therefore, 
about giving 14 days’ notice would not arise in this case. 
Now, if wo considci' the various appointment letters and 
the period.s mentioned therein, it appears that the work¬ 
man concenicd was employed for Itxed periods and that 
too purely a.s a temporary chawkidar. It cannot be argued 
that the workman concerned wus a permanent workman. 
Then it has also not been proved though alleged that be¬ 
cause the workman concerned requested to make him per¬ 
manent, be was discharged. On the contrary, the docii- 
mentai-y us well as the oral evidence amply goes to show 
that the workman concerned was discharged only because 
the period for which he was appointed was over and 
thereafter his services were not required. The question 
of considering him for another fresh appointment also 
does not arise inasmuch as for such a post one .should be 
a non-matric oi- non S.S.C. while the workman concerned 
has passed B. Com. by now. Of course, this is not i ground 
either to terminate his services nor a ground for not taking 
him as afresh but one thing is cenain that to secure 
a job of tempornry watchman the workman concerned 
hud Used ford means and he had gone to the length of 
mentioning his qualifications wrongly and that too with 
mala fide intention In my opinion, therefore, though this 
is not a ground to terniiiiate his services, the Bank would 
be justified in not faking such a person even as afre.sh 
when he ha.s made such a wrong statements in his applica¬ 
tion itself. In institutions like Banks persons of this type 
cannot be tolerated, So In my opinion this is also not a 
case of being lenient. In my view, therefore, tlje action of 
the Bank is quite legal and proper and the reference 
deserves to be rejected. 

12. The result is that the reference is rejected. No orders 
■as to costs. 

Ahmedahad.

The case of the workmen is that by virtue of Coking Coal 
Mines (Nationalrsationj Act, 1972 of Coking Coal Mines aloflg 
with coke oven plants in or belonging to the mines also 
stood nationalised. In addition 12 speedieu coke oven piaiiis 
not belonging to the owners of the mines but known to exist 
near about the mine weie also nationalised. Coke oven plants 
propularly known as Sanjib Coke oven of Sanjib Coke Manu- 
tacturing Company also stood narionalised in item No. 9 
of the second schedule of the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) 
Act and titie, interests right and the niaiidgement vested into 
the Ceiiiiul Uoveuiiicnt after ihe nalionalisatien and in due 
course it was ttasferied to M/s. BCCL by the Central Gov¬ 
ernment. It was decided by the Supreme Court ni the case 
of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. vrs. M/s. BCCL leported 
in 1983 Supreme Court case (vtiume I page 147) that the 
said Coke oven plants belonged to Sanjib Coke Co. i.e. 
Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. before the nationalisation. 
The Sanjib Coke Manufactuiing Co. who were the owners 
of Sanjib Coke oven plants filed Writ Petitions in the Calcutta 
High Court challenging inclusion of their coke oven plant 
in the second .schedule of the Act. The Hon ble High Court, 
Calcutta appointed Shn R. N. Sharma the then Managing 
Director of M/s. BCCL and Shri P, D. Ajmera partner ot 
Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. as Joint teceivers to operate 
the coke oven plant of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. till 
the disposal of the Writ peliiion vide order dated 30-3-72. 
Against the said order an appeal was filed before the Hon'ble 
High Court Ca'culta and the appellate Court confirmed the 
order of appointments of Joint Receivers .and further ordered 
that Shri Um Prakash Gupta the Chief Administrative Officer 
of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. was to work as the Mana- 
ger under the joint receivership. The joint receivers centinued 
the busine.ss of Sanjib Coke 'I ■ (■ ■ engaged 

workers and paid wages to t i . and the 

business of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co, under the joint 
receivership continued upto 10-12-1982 when the Supreme 
Court decided and di.sposed off the writ petition on being 
transferred to it from the Calcutta High Court holding that 
the Nationalisation Act of 1972 was a valid piece of legis¬ 
lation. Immediately after the said judgment dated 10-12-1982 
by the Supreme Court, M/s. BCCL took over the physical 
possession of the said coke oven plants appointed the security 
guards to look after it. The management of Sanjib Coke 
Manufacturing Co. was evading to hand over the full chaige 
of the p’ants and machineries books of accounts and other 
materials and on application being made by M/s. BCCL, 
the Supreme Court was plead to direct Shri P. D. Ajmera 
one of the joint receivers to hand over the possession of 
plants and machineries, books of accounts etc. to M/s. BCCL 
vide Its order dated 29-7-83 and accordingly Shri Ajmera 
handed over the charge of the coke plants. Theiealter M/s. 
BCCL took over possession of the said coke oven plants 
along with all its assets and liabilities. 

Sanjeeb Coke oven is one of the big p’anls of the locality 
situated over large area of land. It manufacture^ hard coke 
and employs more than 200 workers for maiiuldctunng 
hard coke. The average production of hard coke of the 
company was 50 tonnes per day v/hile it was in operation. 
M/s. BCCL took charge Of the said coke oven just after 
the decision of the Supte.me Court on lQ-12-83 holding the 
validity of the nationalisation Act but instead of continuing 
the operation of production of hard coke u.s usual being done 
during the regime of Joint Kcceivers, M/s. BCCL stopped 
the production and management of the workers of the said 
coke oven without any notice or information. Since then 
M/s. BCCL has deliberaiely shut operation of the s.aid coke 
oven for some idicrior purpose. Piioi to and during the 
management of Joint Receivers moie than 200 workers were 
on the roll of the coke oven plant who weie employed for 
the production of hard coke. The erstwhile employer namely 
Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. was exploiting the workers 
by paying them much less wages than what was legitimately 
payable to them. Most of the workers were being paid a 
consolidated sum of Rs. 5,25 P. per day and thus they were 
deprived of even the minimum wages. The workmen had 
to work on the meagure wages on account of dirth of jobs 
elsewhere. The erstwhile employer had adopted variou.s de¬ 
vices to deprive the workers of their legitimate dues. They 
were not paid proper wages, bonus and were not being made 
the members of the Coal Mines Provident Fund. Their service 
conditions were not maintained properly according to the

rules and regulations. The aforesaid malpractices continued 
even during the period of joint receivership. The erstwhile 
employers and the joint receivers did not ma main the proper 
records of the workers engaged in running the coke oven 
plants in order to deprive the workmen the benefits of vari¬ 
ous provision of law rules, awaids and agreement. 

A complaint was made on behalf of the woikmen of Mys. 
Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. to the authority of the 
Labour Department and Government of Bihar, Dhanbad re¬ 
garding not maintaining proper documents and not issuing 
employmet cards to their workers and thereafter the emp¬ 
loyers issued employment cards to the concerned workmen 
showing them as employees of Ahmed contractors. The 
erstwhile employers and even the joint receiver used to show 
the employment of mo.st of the workers under contractor 
a'though there was no such independent contractor. The 
concerned workmen of the present re'erence were being shown 
employed by the Coke oven plant through one Ekbal AhmeJ 
as a con'raclor. The said Ekb-il Ahmad neier acted as a 
contractor and there was no contract between Ekbal Ahmed 
and tire erstwhile employer or the .Toint Receivers of the 
said coke oven plants. Ekbal Ahmed is himielf an employee 
of M|s. BCCL woiking at different collieries. The ..cmcc ned 
workmen have all along teen in the direct employment ot 
the owners of M/s. .Sanjeev Coke Manufacturing Co., the 
Joint Ifeceivers and subsequently of M/s. BCCL. Moreover 
the job performed by the concerned workmen as mentioned 
in the schedule to the order of inference cannot be entrusted 
to a contractor. The intermediate contractor was a farce 
entity posed as employer to deprive the concerned workman 
of the various benefits which aie .available to them as legular 
and direct employee of the principal employer. If the veil is 
lifted in the present case it will be found that Ekbal Ahmed 
was never an independent contractor and M/s. Sanjeev Coke 
Manufacturing Company was ail along the direct employer 
of the concerned workman. The control and supervision of 
the work of the concerned workman were done directly by 
tlie owners of Ms. Sanjeev Coke Manufacturing Company 
and the joint receivers through their manager. 

M/s, BCCL stopped the operation of the coke oven plants 
)inmedaitely after taking over the possession and management 
on the aprehension that a large number of persons are 
claiming employment. The action of the management of M/s. 
BCCL in stopping the operation of the Coke Oven Plants and 
thereby stopping the employment of the work of regular 
workers was brought to the notice of RCMS which was the 
union of the concerned workmen. The RCMS took up the 
case of the concerned workmen who were rendered unem¬ 
ployed by M/s. BCCL and it was agreed by the management 
of BCCL that they would provide employment to all those 
workmen who would be found .genuine after scrutiny. After 
decision of the Supreme Court on 10-12-1982 M/s, BCCL 
constituted a screening committee to scrutinise the particulars 
of the persons claiming employment and for the same notice 
was issued advising the workers to appear before the inter¬ 
view board on 15-11-83 for interview and for scrutinising 
their particulars to find their penuinness for their employ¬ 
ment by M/s. BCCL. In response to the aforesaid notice 
many persons including the concerned workmen appeared 
before the interview board who were interviewed and their 
identities were found genuine proving they were emplyoees 
of M/s. Sanjeev Coke Manufacturing Co. working on theii 
coke oven plants before take over bv M/s. BCCL. The 
interview hoard prepared the records of the interview and 
submitted a report to the authority of M/s. BCCL holdin.g 
that the concerned workmen are genuine employees and 
their employment deserves to he regularised. Tn spite of the 
said finding M/s. BCCL did not regularise the services of 
the concerned workmen and did not start the production of 
hard coke from the said coke oven p'ants. The concerned 
workmen are internally starvin,g being t'ut (f employment 
since the stoppage of their vvork and the management of 
M/s. BCCL has failed to implement and .act according to 
the provision of the nationalisation Act. According to the 
provision made in Section 17 of the Coking Coal Mine's 
(Nationalisation) Act, 1972 every person who is workman 
within the meaning of the I.D. Act. 1947 and has been 
immediately before the appointed day in the employment of 
Coking Coal Mines^o_r C 9 kc oven.s plants shaT become oft, 
and from the’ appointed 'day an employee of- Hie • Centra) 


Government or, as the case may be, of the Government 
company. The concerned workmen were on the rolls of the 
coke oven plants and they became the employees of the said 
coke oven plant automatically after the nationalisation irres¬ 
pective of tile fact whether they were in direct employment 
of M/.^. Sanjeev Manufacturing Co. or of any so called con¬ 
tractor. The concerned woikmen finding no alternative raised 
an industrial dispute through their union RCMS before the 
ALCfCl, Dhanbad. The management of Ms. BCCL appear¬ 
ed before the ALC(C) in conciliation. The conciliation failed 
and on failure report being sent to the Government of India, 
Ministry of Labour the present reference was made to this 
Tribunal for adjudication. The only plea of the management 
of M/s. BCCL was that there was no valid document to 
support the claim of the concerned workmen. The particulais 
of the concerned workmen was thoroughly scrutinised by the 
officers of M/s. BCCL themselves and the screening commit¬ 
tee was satisfied regarding the genuineness of the concerned 
workmen. When duly constituted screening committee of 
M/s. BCCL found the genuineness of the employment of 
ilie concerned workmen, there can be no basis whatsoever to 
contend iiiat the identity of the concerned workmen were not 
duly proved. On the above facts it has been prayed that 
the demand of the workmen of the reference is pioper and 
justified and that they are entitled resumption of duties re- 
gularisation of their services and payrnent of their wages by 
the management of M/s. BCCL since the date they took 
over charge of the management ot Coke oven .plants in 
question. 

The case of the management is ih.'t the concerned work¬ 
men had made no demand diiccily to the management. The 
workmen referred in the annexnre to the order of reference 
are absolute strangers and tlierc was at no time any employer 
employee relationship between them ami tlic manajement or 
its predecessors. Ihe said workmen were never employed by 
the Sanjeev Coke Manufacturing Co. The coke oven rian‘s 
owned by Sanjeev Coke maiafacturing Co. liad been ilcsed 
towards the end of 1982 when it was .still in the possession 
of Shri P. D. Ajmera in the capacity of Joint Receiver and 
as such their can neither be any industrial dispute nor any 
industrial adjudication of any such purported dispute with 
respect to a dosed industrial establishment. 

The Coking Coal Mines and Coke oven plants covered by 
the first and second schedule of the Coking Coal Mines 
(N.afionalisatiori) Act, 1972 which came into force from 
1-5-1972 were transferred by the Central Government to 
M/s. BCCL established for the purpose of managing these 
coking coal mines and coke oven plants and BCCL therefore 
became the owner of these mdustrial establishment. With a 
view to defeat and delay and taking over of the aforesaid 
plants the previous owner of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. 
filed a Writ Petition before the Hon'b'e Calcutta High Court 
contending that the said Coke plants had not vested in the 
Central Government or BCCL because the act ulteravires 
and secondly because coke oven plants in question was not 
including in the relevant schedule of the Act and succeeded 
ill getting an order for appointment of ioint receivers over 
the said plants and thus the union of India and BCCL were 
prevented from taking over the possession of the said plant. 
The Wilt Petition of M/s. Sanjib Coke Manufacturing Co. 
which was filed in Ca'cutta High Court was transferred to 
the Hon'ble Supreme Court and numbered ns transferred 
case No. 1 of 1980. At the time of transfer Shri P. D. 
Aimera aiorig vvith director of BCCJ. were the joint leceocis 
ot the said coke oven plants i.nder the order of the Hon'ble 
Calcutta High Court. By order ,dated 10-12-82 a constitution 
bench of the Hon'ble Supreme Court held that the impugned 
Acts were valid and the aforesaid plan's vested in the Union 
of India and consequently in BCCL. While the petition was 
pending in the Calcutta Hi.ch Court, the Dirvisiori bench 
of the said Court while confirming the order of the Single 
Judge appointing joint receiver directed that Shri Om Prakash 
Gupta an officer of M/s. Saniib Coke Manufacturing Co. 

iil Act a.s Manager under the two joint receivers and take 
direcfii'n from both the Joint Receivers to manage the plant. 
Shri P. D. Aimera one of the Toint Receivers who was 
-actually in possession and managing coke plant in question 
was running the business of the svid plant ns sole proprietor 
in collusion with the said Manaegr even after the orders of 
the Hon’b'e Calcutta High Court. When the Director of

Point No. 1 and 2 

Ihese two points have been taken up tooethei for discus¬ 
sion as they are interconnected. 

Admittedly Sanjib Coke Oven of Sanjib Coke Manufactur¬ 
ing company was nationalised by virtue of Coking Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act, 1972 and the title, interest right and 
the management vested in the Central Government after the 
nationalisation and in due course it was transferred to M/s. 
BCCL by the Central Government. It is also admitted that 
the owners of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing company filed a 
Writ Petition in the Hon’ble High Court of CalciUta challeng¬ 
ing the nationalisation of the Coke oven plant and Sanjib 
Coke Manufacturing company and that the Hon’ble High 
Court of Calcutta appointed Shri R, N. Sharma the then 
Managing Director of M/s, BCCL and Shri P. D. Ajmera 
partner of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing company as Joint 
Receivers to operate the Coke oven plants of Sanjib Coke 
manufacturing company till the disposal of the Writ petition 
vide order dafed 30-3-72. Against the said order an appeal 
was filed before the Hon'ble High Court at Calcutta and 
the appellate Court confirmed the order of appointment of 
Joint Receivers and further ordered that Shri Om Prakash 
Gupta the then Administrative Officer of Sanjib Coke Manu¬ 
facturing company was to work as the Manager under the 
Jomt Receivers. It is further admitted that the Writ petition 
pend ng before the High Court of Calcutta was transferred 
to the Hon'ble Supreme Court and the Hon’ble Supreme Court 
was placed to decide and dispose off the Writ petition holding 
that the Coking Coal Mines fNationalisation) Act, 1972 was 
a valid piece of legislation vide its order dated 10-12-82. S' 
far the case of both the parties are admitted, According 
to the workmen after the said judgement of the Hon'ble 
Supreme Court dated 10-12-82 M/s, BCCL took over physical 
possession of the coke oven plant but arcudine to the M/s. 
BCCL the possession of the Sanjib Coke Oven-plant was not 
given by Shri P, D. Ajmera who in fact was in possession 
of the coke oven plant. According to M/s BCCL, the 
management of Sanjib Coke Manufacturing company was 
evading to hand over the full charge of the plants and machi¬ 
neries books of accounts etc. and as such M/s. BCCL filed 
a case before the Supreme Court and thereafter the Hon’ble 
Supreme Court was pleased to direct Shri P. D. .M'mera 
one of the loint Receiver to hand over the possession of 
plants and machineries, books of accounts etc. vide its 
Order dated 29-7-83 and thereafter Shri Ajmera handed over 
charge of the coke oven plant to M/s. BCCL on 20-9-83. 
According to the workmen M/s. BCCL came in physical 
possession of Coke Oven plant immediately after judgement 
of the Hon’ble Supreme Court dated 10-12-82 whereas accord¬ 
ing to M/s. BCCL it came in possession of the coke oven 
plant on 20 9-83 after the order of the Hon’ble Supreme Court 
dated 29-7-83 by which Shri Ajmera was directed to hand 
over charge of Coke Oveu plant along with the books of 
accounts etc. to BCCL. 


In para-11 of the W.S. of the workmen it is staled that 
M/s. BCCL took charge of the Coke oven plant just after 
the decision of the Supreme Court on 10-12-82 holding the 
validity of the natiolisation Act, but instead of continuing 
the operation and production of hard coke as was being done 
during the regime of Joint Receivers M/s. BCCL stopped the 
prednetion and engagement of the workers of the said coke 
oven plants without anv notice. It will thus appear as 
stated by the workmen that Sanjib Coke Oven plant stopped 
production in the month of December, 1982. WW-1 Ram 
Sakai Ram and WW-2 Aftab Ahmed who are two of the 
concerned persons in the reference have stated that they had 
worked till 15-12-82 and that on 15-12-82 the work of the 
coke oven was stopped by the officers of BCCl.. Thus it is 
the definite case of the workmen that Saniib Coke oven 
was closed in December, 1982. It is the admitted case of 
the parties that Sanjib Coke oven was ur.rler the Recei''ership 
during the pendency of the Writ petition in which Ihe Coking 
Coal Mines (Nationarsation) Act was challenged and that the 
said Wiit petition was decided bv the Hon’ble Supreme 
Court on 10-12-82. it is evident therefore that prior to 
10-12 82 .idmilledly M/s. BCCL was not in possession of 
Sanjib Coke oven plant and that the said plant was under the 
Joint Receivership appointed by Calcutta High Court. 


Now the question is whether M/s. BC( f came in 
possession of Sanjib Coke oven plant soon after the decision 
of Hon’ble Supreme Couit dated 10-12-82. Ext. 1\I-1 is the 
application for direction to the Joint Receiver to hand over 
the possession and management of the business and 
property of the coRe oven plants which was numbered as 
Civil Mis. Petition No. 11026 yf 1982 in the Hon'ble 
Supreme Court of India wherein if is prayed that 
Shri P. D. Ajmera be directed to hand over at Dhanbad 
the possession and management of the business and pro¬ 
perty of the Coke Oven plant to M/s. BCCL along with 
all assets and books of accounts etc. Ext, M-2 is the 
order passed in Civil Misc. petition No. 11026 of 1983 
by the Hon’ble Supreme Court dated 29-7-83. The order 
shows that Shri P. D. Ajmera was directed to haiidover pos¬ 
session of all the requisite possession of which he took 
over when he was appointed as Joint Receiver and then 
hand over possession to the Chief Judicial Manager of M/s. 
BCCL. It is clear therefore that as Shri P. D. Ajmeia one 
of the Receivers of the Coke oven plants had not handed 
over possession and management of Sanjib Coke oven, 
he was directed by the Hon’ble Supreme Court after 
making full deliberation to deliver possession and the manage¬ 
ment of the coke oven plants to M/s, BCCL through its 
Finance Officer. MW-1 Shri R. G. Singh who was, Dy. 
Chief Personnel Manager, Incharge. Man power, at the head¬ 
quarters of BCCL stated that BCCL was not allowed to play 
Ihe role of Joint Receiver by the erstwhile management during 
the Joint Receivership. It will appear that Shri P. D. Ajmera 
who was one of the partners of Sanjib Coke oven 
was appointed as one of the Receivers and Shri Om Piakash 
Gupta who was the Administrative Officer of Sanjib Manu¬ 
facturing Co. was appointed Manager by the Hon’ble Calcutta 
High Court during the pendency" of the Writ petition and 
it can well be imagined that the persons who were already 
in possession and management of the Coke oven plants were 
given the power to manage the operation of the Coke plant 
and as such it appears quite probably that Shri Om Prakash 
Gupta and Shri P. D. Ajmera were not allowing the officers 
of BCCL tO' work as Joint Receiver and the order of the 
Hon’ble Supreme Court in Ext. M-2 is clear indication 
(hat Shri P. D. Ajmera and Shri Om Prakash Gupta were 
not allowing BCCL to take possession and management ot 
the Coke oven plants after the decision of the Supreme 
Court dated 10-12-82. There is no positive evidence on the 
part of the workmen that M/s. BCCL had taken physical 
possession and management of the Coke oven plants after 
10-12-82 or prior to the order dated 29-7-83 passed by the 
Hon bh Supreme Court. In view of the evidence discussed 
above 1 hold that M/s. BCCL had ncu come in possession 
of the managemen! of Sanjib Coke Oven prior to 29-7-83. 


As discussed above it will appear from the case of the 
parties that Sanjib Coke oven was closed in December, 
1982. This fact is further supported by Ext. M-35 
daled 19-2-83 is letter from Shri R. N. Sharma, 
Joint Secretary of RCMS to the Chairmaii-eum-Managing 
Director M/s. BCCL KoyaJu Bhawan, Dhanbad lelatiug to 
the employment of lh.- workmen of Sanjib Coke Manufactur¬ 
ing Company, It is seated in this letter that the private 
management has clo ed the Coke oven and have filed away 
and that the management of M/s. BCCL has also not taken 
over possession of the sa d coke oven plant and as such about 
90 workmen who were encaged in the coke oven in different 
jobs are idle since 16 12-82. Thus it is clear from that this 
letter of Shri R. N. Sharma, loint Secretary of RCVIS who 
appears to be in know of the situation that Sanjib Coke oven 
had been closed by the erstwhile owner of Sanjib Coke oven 
and that M/s. BCCL had not taken its possession. Ti clearly 
supports the facts fhar the coke oven plants was closed by 
the erstwhile owner of Sanjib Coke oven prior to 16-12-82. 
1 hold therefore that Sanjib Coke oven plant was closed by 
the piivate erstwhile management prior to 16-12-82. 

T have held above that Sanjfis Coke oven plant was closed 
prior to 16-12-82 by the owner of Saniib Coke Oven and 
that the management of M/s. BCCL took its possession and 
management in a closed condition after 29-7-83. 

T fuither hold that the Coke Oven plant of Sanjeev Coke 
Manufacluring Company W'as in a closed condiiion at the 
time BCCl took its possession Thus po ni Nos 1 and 2 
are accordingly decided.

MW-2, Dipankar Gupta is working os Dv. Chief Engi¬

appear that the only reliable evidence in respect of the

anpear in Ext. M-38 containing the list of the workers of

workm.g in .Sanieev Coke Oven as on 1.5-12-1982 

^ I hold therefore that the concerned 71 persons nametl

were not thr departmental workem of Sanieev Coke Manu¬

515 


[vir.r li—=3’? ;t (ii)J i;T^r i7, layo/Ttm 3S, lyii 


I have held above that only the concemed 71 persons 
HLimed in the list in hxt, M-38 -weie the contractors wor¬ 
kers Ekbal Ahmed who were engaged in Sdnjeev Coke 
tJvcn lor charging and discharging ot bhatta and that the 
other concerned persons were neither the contractois wor¬ 
kers nor the workmen of Sanjeev Coke Manufacuring 
Company. The points which now arise is whether the 
concerned 71 persons who were contractors workers wcie 
in fact the workmen of hanjeev Coke Oven piani. fhe 
workmen have based their case on the decision reported 
in 1978 (11. LLJ Page 397) Hussainbhai’s case, In order 
U) appreciate the point 1 briefly state that Sa'njeev Coke 
Oven plant was a behivc coke producing only hard coke. 
'I he main work, v.as the production of hard coke and the 
said hard coke was made Iiom coal after cliargin^ and 
discharging in the Bhatta and the said job of charging and 
dischaigiug of the Bhatta were done by the 71 coneerned 
persons as held by me. It is no doubt admitted that the 
concerned persons were not the direct employees ot Sanjeev 
Coke Oven and that the owners of Sanjeev Coke Oven 
were getting cliarging and discharging of the bhatta through 
contractors workmen. I'heir Lordship have laid down in 
Hussainbhai’s case the true test as to who will be the real 
employer in such a case—whether the management or the 
intermediate contractor. Their Lordhips held that the true 
lest is whether a worker or group of workers labours to 
produce goods and these goods or services are for the busi¬ 
ness of another, that other is, in faci^ emplycr. He has 
economic control over the workers subsistence, skill and 
continued employment and if the managcnient for any 
reason chokes off, the worker is virtually laid off. The 
presepcf of inteimcdiate contractors with whom alone the 
workers have immediate or direct relationship is of no 
consequence, when on lifting the veil or looking of the 
conspectus of factors governing the employment, wc discover 
the naked truth though draped in ditferent paper arrange¬ 
ment, that (he real employer is the management not the 
immediate contractor. Their Lordships held that the 
Court must be astute to avoid the mischief and achieve the 
purpose of law and not be mislead by the maya of legal 
appearance. It v/ill appear that the job being performed 
by the contracioi.s workers was an integral part of tlte 
manufacture of the coke for which the coke oven was estab¬ 
lished. 

Every worker who works for the piincipul employer to 
whom the provision of contractor labour Act are attracted, 
has to he treated as the worker of the principal employer 
unless 2 conditions arc satisfied :— 

(1) Tliat the establishment liad secured a certificate 
of registcrulion for tlte relevant period and 

(2) It hud employed contract labour through a licen¬ 
ced colractor. 

If either of the conditions arc missing then the contract 
labour employed through the contractor shall be treated to 
be the “worker” o fthe employer. Admittedly in the pic- 
sence case Contractor Ekbal Ahmed hud not obtained any 
licence to work us cuntracter There is also no evidence 
to the effect that the management of Sanjeev Coke Oven 
had obtained a ceriiticatc of registration for the relevant 
period. In the present case therefore neither of the 2 con¬ 
ditions arc specified and as such the 71 contractor labour 
employed by the manugemenl of Sanjeev Coke Oven 
throuf^ the contractor Ekbal Ahmed has to be treated as 
the Worker of the employer. In the above view of the 
matter 1 liold that 71 concerned persons were the workmen 
of the Sanjeev Coke Oven ol Sanjeev Coke Manufacturing 
Company. Accordingly I hold that there was relationship 
of employer and employee between Sanjeev Coke Manu¬ 
facturing Company and the concerned 71 persons. 


Point No. 6 

It appears from the evidence that the concerned 71 per¬ 
sons were working in Sanjeev' Coke Oven plant at the time 
the Sanjeev Coke Oven plant was nationalised and they 
were working even till l.S-12-1982. The Hon’blc Supreme 
Court held that the nationalisation of the Coke Oven plant 


was constitutional and valid piece of legislation btit the- 
possession and management of the Sanjeev Coke Overt 
was not handed over to M)s. BCCL until a decision by 
the Hon’hlc Supreme Court directing Shri Ajmei'a to hand 
over possession dated 29-7-1983 and its aciual posse- 
ssioq being taken by M^s. BCCL on 20-y-19'83. It wiU 
thu.s apjicar that the concerned 71 persons altliough con¬ 
tractors workers were the workmen of Sanjeev Coke Oven 
at the time of its nationalisation and they continued to 
Work in. Sanjeev Coke Oven during the period of Kecciver- 
ship pending the Writ petition and that their work was 
stopped in Uecember, 1982. ITius it is clear that the con¬ 
cerned 71 persons were working at the time of nationalisa¬ 
tion and also after nationalisation. 1 have held above that 
the employment of the concerned 71 persons under the 
contractor has to be taken as their omploymenf under the 
management of Simjeev Coke Oven. In accordance with 
the Coking Coal Mine (Nationalisation) Act the concerned 
persons therefore will be deemed to continue as workmen 
of the management of BCCL as they were workmen of 
Sanjeev Coke Oven on the date of nationalisation. 

Another fatlor has cropped in. Although the concerned 
persons were working at the time of nationalisation of Cote 
Oven plant they were stopped from 15-12-1982 and at that 
time M/s. BCCL had not taken possession and the manage¬ 
ment of Sanjeev Coke Oven plant. M]s, BCCL came in 
actual possession on 20-9-1983. Thus although the con¬ 
cerned persons were working in Sanjeev Coke Oven at the 
time of nationalisation the Coke Oven plant was closed in, 
December, 1982 and the management of BCCL came in 
possession of Ihe management of Sanjeev Coke Oven on 
20-9-1983. It will appear that BCCL came m possession 
of closed establishment. It has been held in 1957 (1) 
LU—^235 Pipniich Sugar Mills vrs. Pipraich Sugar, Mills 
Madoor Union) and m 1960 (11 ELI page 1) (Hnthcr 
Singh Munulacturing Co. Ltd.,—^vrs—Union of India) that 
there can neither be any industrial dispute nor any indus¬ 
trial adjudication with respect to the closed indusUial estab¬ 
lishment. As the Sanjeev Coke Oven plant has been dosed 
towards the end of 1982 when it was still in possession of 
Shri P. D. Ajmei'a in the capacity of Joint Receiver, there 
can be no dispute between the workmen of Sanjeev Coke 
Oven plant and the management of M/s. BCCL as the 
said industrial establishment was closed when M/s. BCCL 
came in possession and the management of Sanjeev Coke 
Oven plant, 

It will also appear from the notes of the Director of 
Personnel in Ext. M-IJ dated 12-2-1985 that the manage¬ 
ment ol BCCL had decided that the .said coke plant was 
not required to be run by BCCL. T bus it appears that 
Sanjeev Coke Oven was closed in December, 1982 and the 
present management of M/s. BCCL do ndt intend to re¬ 
start the said coke oven plant as.it i.s uneconomical to start 
the said coke oven. It is now 199(> and BCCL has not 
actually stalled working Sanjeev Coke Oven. V appears 
therefore that M /s. BCCL genuinely feels that Coke Oven 
plant Is not to be reopened for the manufacture of coke. 
Taking all these factors into consideration f hold that thet® 
can be no industrial dispute in respect of the closeij San¬ 
jeev Coike Oven plant and as such the concerned 71 per¬ 
sons. cannot be regularised or allowed to resqmo duties In 
a closed industrial establishment. 

1 hold therefore that the concerned 71 persons cannot 
be allowed to resume duties and regularisation on the rolls 
of BCCL, 

The concerned persons had not worked in Sanjeev Coke- 
Oven after M /s. BCCL took its possession and the manage- 
ment on 20-9-1983 and as such the workmen cannot claim 
arrears of wages for the period of their idleness and diffe¬ 
rence of wagis for the period of their previous service 
under Sanjeev Manufacturing Company from M /s. BCCL. 

in ilic resuli, 1 hold that the demand of RCMS that the 
management of M/a. BCCI.. should allow resumption of 
duties by regul'arisation on their rolls, the concerned 184 
persons, who claim to have 'worked previously in Sanjeev

for one year is justified ? If not, to \sliBt relief

Coke Oven taken over by BCCL, after nationalisation, and 
pay them wages for the period of their idleness as well as 
difference of wages said to be due to them for the period

they are entitled to no relief. 

This is my Award.

New Delhi, the 30th January, 1990 

S.O. 433.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 

hereby publishes the award of the Central Government 
Industrial Tribunal (No. 2), Dhanbad as shown in the Anne- 
xuro in the industrial dispute between the employers in relation 
to the management of Kankanee Open Cast project of Sijua 
Area V of M/s. BCCL and their workmen, which was recei¬ 
ved by the Central Government on 22-1-1990.

BEFORE THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL

Shri I. N. Sinha, Presiding Officer.

In the matter of an industrial dispute Section 10(1) (d) of 
the I. D. Act, 1947 

parties : 


The case of the workmen is that the concerned workmen 
S/Shri Ganesh Chandra Rikshit and Om Prakash Mondal 
were appointed as trainee auto Electrician vide letter dated 
30-1-82 for one year training as Auto Electrician on consoli¬ 
dated wages of Rs. 17.20 P. only. The concerned workman 
Shri Om Prakash Mondal joined on 1-3-82 and Shri Ganesh 
Chandra Rikshit on 20-2-82, The two concerned workmen 
were regularised in Group-E as Auto Electrician Helper by 
letter dated 15-12-83 after completion of their training for one 
year. The concerned workman Shri Rikshit had completed 
one year of training on 19-2-83 and Shri Mondal had comple¬ 
ted one year training on 24-2-83 and as such they should have 
been regularised with effect from 20-2-83 and 25-2-83 respec¬ 
tively as Auto Electrician in Group-D. The two concerned 
workmen were transferred to Kankanee Colliery of Sijua Area 
with effect from 28-9-83. The concerned workmen requesteu 
for their regularisation as Auto Electrician in Group-D and 
they were given assurance by the management of Jogta Fire 
Project that their case for regularisation has been forwarded 
to the Area Office and that on receipt of the order they 
would be regularised as auto Electrician. The concerned 
workman were wrongly regularised as Auto Electrician 
Helper Group-E with effect from 1-12-83 and they were 
wrongly paid the wages of Auto Electrician Helper. The 
concerned workmen had been appointed as Auto Electrician 
trainee and after completion of one year of training 
it was proper that they should be regularised as Auto 
Electrician Helper in Group-D. The concerned workmen were 
independently doing the Auto Electricians job on Shove) 
Dumper, Dozer and drill etc. in Jogta Fire Project and in 
Kankanee Open Cast Project. The concerned workmen several 
times requested the Colliery management for their regulari¬ 
sation a.s Auto Electrician Excavation Group D and to pay 
them the scale of wages of Group-D. Thereafter on 5-10-85 
the concerned workmen filed petition before the Supdf. of 
Kankanee Colliery and thereafter their union, namely, the 
Coal Mines Engineering Workers Association also wrote fof 
their regularisation to the management of Kankanee Colliery 
and the General Manager and Personnel Manager of the Area 
but their demand was not conceded. Thereafter their union 
raised an industrial dispute before the ALC(C), Dhanbad who 
started conciliation proceeding. On failure of the concilia¬ 
tion proceeding the present reference was made to this 
Tribunal by the Government. On the above facts it is prayed 
that the concerned workmen Ganesh Chandra Rakshit and 
Om Prakash Mondal should be regularised as Auto Electrician 
Oroup-D and be paid the wages of Croup-D with effect from 
20-2-83 and 25-2-83 respectively along with other benefits. 


Employers in relation to the management of Kankanee 
Open Cast Project of Sijua Area-V of M/s. B.C.C. 
Ltd. and their workmen. 

APPEARANCES : 

On behalf of the workmen—Shri K. D. Prasad. Secretary 
C.M.E.WI.A. 

On behalf of the employers—Shri G, Prasad, Advocate. 
STATE : Bihar INDUSTRY : Coal 

Dhanbad, the 15th January, 1990 
AWARD 

T^e Government of Jndia, Ministry of Labour in exercise 
Of the powers conferred on them under Section ]0(l)(d) of 
referred the following dispute to this 
Tribraal for adjudication vide their Order No. L-24012/3/87- 
D.IV (Bl, dated, the 24th July, 1987. 

SCHEDULE 

“Wh^her the action of the Management of Kankanee 
Collie^ (Open Cast Project) of Sijua Arca-V of 
M/s. Bharat Coking Coal Ltd., P.0, Bansjora, Di.slf. 
Dhanbad in not regularising S/Sliri Ganesh Chandra 
Kik^it and Om Prakash Mondal as Auto Electrician 
in Excavation Grade-D after completion of TfAinlng 


Ihc case of the management is that the concerned work¬ 
men have been seeking promotion in the garb of the term 
"regularisation” which is not permissible. The promotion is 
a managernent's function and cannot be claimed as a matter of 
right. It is admitted that the two concerned workmen were 
itainee Auto Electrician vide letter dated 
30-1-82 for one year training as Auto Electrician and that 
Om Prakash Mondal joined on 1-3-82 and Shri Rikshit joined 
on 20-2-83. They were regularised in Group-E as Auto Elec¬ 
trician Helper by letter dated 15-12-83 after they completed 
one year training. The management decided that such trainees 
should be trade tested in the respective projects by a com¬ 
mittee to be constituted by the Chief Engineer, Excavation 
depmment with one Supdt. Excavation of Vikash Bhawan, Dy. 
P.M. Excavation Vikash Bhawan, concerned project Manager 
and Executive Engineer of the Project on the recommenda¬ 
tion of the said departmental selection committee such trai- 
nee.s may be regularised in Excavation Group-E as Auto Elec¬ 
trician helper on successful completion of one year service as 
helper. It was also decided that their case of regular place- 
incnt m the appropriate grade will be made after adjudging 
suitability through practical trade test. Immediately after 
completion of one year training the concerned workmen were 
neither eligible nor qualified for regularisation in excavation 
Executive^ Group-D. According to the nmnugement there is 
no merit m the demand of the workmen. It was not a condi- 
rion of employment of the concerned workmen that they shall 
be regularised m Group-I after one year training. On the 
above facts it is prayed that the AwSrd be made in favour 
ot tne management. 



[mn II—ijiw 3 (ii)] 


»nTfT<f:T tnrw; 1 1 , 199 o/ffm 2 8 ,1911 


Sl7 


The point for decision in this case is whether the two 
concerned workmen arc entitled to be placed as Auto Electri¬ 
cian Excavation in Excavation Gronp-D with elfcct from 
ll-2"82. 

Ihe workmen examined two witnesses and the manage¬ 
ment examined one witness in support of their respective 
cases. The documents of the management arc marked Exts. 
M-1 to M-10 and the documents of the workmen are marked 
Ext. W-1 to W-8. 

It is now admitted that the concerned workmen have been 
given auto Electrician Group-D vide Ext. W-1 dated 26-5-1989 
with effect from 30-1-83. It is further stated in the olllcc 
order Ext, W-1 tliat the effective date for financial benefit 
will be on and from 11-11-86 as approved by the competent 
authority. Thus it will appear that although the two con¬ 
cerned worlonen were placed as Auto Electrician Group-L» 
Excavation with effect from 30-1-83 they were not given 
ihe wages of Group-D from 30-1-83 to 10-11-86. The work¬ 
men have however conceded that they would accept their 
regularisation of auto Electrician in Group-D with effect 
from 30-1-83 but they demand that they should also get wages 
of Group-D or the difference of wages of Oroup-E or Group- 
D from 30-1-83 to 10-11-86. MW-1 Shri R. B. P. Sahi work¬ 
ing as Sr. P.O, has stated in his cross-examination that auto 
Electricians are given Group-D Excavation. He has not stated 
any reason as to why the concerned workmen were not given 
the wages of Group-D from 30-1-83 to 10-11-86 although the 
concerned workmen were placed in Groiip-D with effect from 
30-11-83. The office order Ext. W-1 also does not ^ive any 
reason as to why the concerned workmen were not given the 
wages of Group-D from the date they were placed jn Excava 
tion Groiip-D. WW-1 and WW-2 arc the two concerned 
workmen whose evidence will show that ihey have been 
regularised as Auto Electrician in Group-D excavation with 
effect from 30-1-83 but have not been paid the wages of 
Group-D with effect from 30-1-83. They have stated that their 
demand is for payment of wages of Group-D from the date 
they had completed one year of the period of then training 
as Auto Electrician. It will also appear from liis evidence 
that although they were regularised as Auto Electrician helper 
they continued working as Auto Electrician in Kankanee 
Colliery Open Cast Project. Ext. W-3 and W-4 ore the 
appointment letters of the two concerned workmen which 
show that they were appointed to the post of Trainee auto 
Electrician for a i^riod of one year and as such they should 
have been regularised as Auto Electrician after their success¬ 
ful training. Ext. WI-6 is a circular dated 14/15-10-86 issued 
by the Addl. Chief Personnel Manager (MP and R) Directo¬ 
rate of Personnel, Koyala Bhawan to all General Manager 
which shows that on completion of one year period of train¬ 
ing the auto Electricians (trainee) working m the O.C.Ps in 
Auto side of HEMM like Dovers, dumper etc. will be regulari¬ 
sed as auto Electrician in Excavation Grade and that such 
of the Electrician of Excavation Grade who were being 
treated as auto Electrician need not have statutory qualifica¬ 
tion, Thus although the circular was made in October, 1986 
it shows that on completion of one year period of training, 
the auto Electricians trainee working in the O.C.P. will be 
regularised as Auto Electrician in Excavation Gradc-D. It is 
clear, therefore, that the concerned workmen .,hould be placed 
as Auto Electrician in Excavation Gradc-D after completion 
of one year period of training us .Auto Electrician trainee. 
It appears that it was because of this circular that the con¬ 
cerned workmen were regularised as .Auto Electrician in 
Group-D after they had completed one year period of ihclr 
training. The workmen have submitted that the manage¬ 
ment had discriminated in not regularising them as Auto 
Electrician in Group-D from the very beginning and had 
placed them previously as Auto Electrician helper in Group-E 
although the management in the case of other Auto Electri¬ 
cian had placed them in Group-D after completion of one 
year pci'iod of their training. Ext, M-7 is another letter from 
the Dy, Chief Personnel Manager from Directorate of Per¬ 
sonnel Koyala Bhawan which shows that while some of the 
areas on completion of one year period of training have re¬ 
gularised them in Excavation Oradc-F remaining areas have 
given Ihem Excavation Grade-D which has created serious 
inconsistency in rc.guhu'isniion between ditrercnl Areas. It 
further states that the- matter has been examined and it has 
been decided that siiovcl orierators, Dumper Operators etc, 
should be extended to regul.'ir scale of Excavation and 
should be given Groiip-D on completion of one year train¬ 
ing. It appears therefore that formerly there was confusion 


in the different collieries about the placement of the Auto 
Electrician trainee, the sovel operators ajid others working 
in the Excavation which was resolved by the management by 
their circulars and ihey were placed in Excavation Grude-D 
after completion of the period of the training, The workmen 
have referred to the office order Ext. W-8 dated 22/23-3-88. 
It show.s that Auto Electricians of East Bii.sseriya Colliery were 
regularised in Excavation Grade-D with effect from the date 
they had completed one year of training as Auto Electrician 
and there was no clause in it that the effective date for finan¬ 
cial benefit will be from any subsequent date as has been 
stated in Ihe case of the concerned workmen in Ext. W-1. 
Ext. W-8 will show that the 4 Auto Electricians were allowed 
Excavation Group-D with effect from the date of completion 
of one year of their Irainmg. It is clear theieforc that the 
management has discriminated in the case of the concerned 
workmen in refusing to pay them the difference of wages of 
Excavation Group E and D from 30-1-83 to 10-11-86. The 
management ostensibly had no reason to withheld the pay¬ 
ment of difference of wages to the concerned workmen from 
30-1-83 to 10-11-86 when the management had placed them 
in Excavafion Group-D with effect from 30-1-83, In the 
above view of the matter the management has to pay the 
wages of Group-D/difference of wages of Gronp-E and 
Group-D to the two concerned workmen from 30-1 83 
to 10-11-86. 

As the workmen have accepted their placement in Excava¬ 
tion Gradc-D as Auto Electrician with effect from 30-1-83. 
now there is no dispute regarding their legularisation in 
Excavation Group-D. 

The W.S. and iJso in the evidence the concerned work¬ 
men have stated that the Tribuna] should promote them in 
Excavation Grade-C in as much us some of the Auto Electri¬ 
cians in Gronp-D who were theii- colleagues have already 
been promoted to Group-C. The scope of the schedule to the 
order of reference is confined to Ine decision whether the 
management was justified in not regularising the concerned 
workmen as Auto Electrician in Excavation Gjoup-D after 
completion of tia'ning for one year and there is no reference 
made to me in the schedule to Ihe reference that I should 
also decide whether the concerned workman should be promo¬ 
ted to Excavation Group-C, Moreover the matter of pi emo¬ 
tion is management's prerogative in which several facts have to 
be looked into such as number of years of experience, qualifi¬ 
cation and whether any vacancy exist.s in the higher Grade-C. 
The matter of promotion is not before me and hence I refrain 
from discussing and passing any order on the same. 

In the result, 1 hold that the action of the management 
of Kankanee Colliery of Sijua Area No. V of M/s. 
BCCL in not regularising the concerned workmen S/Shri 
Ganesh Chandra Rikshit and Om Prakash Mundal as Auto 
Electrician in Excavation Group-D after completion of train¬ 
ing of one year was not justified. However, the manage¬ 
ment has now regularised the concerned workmen by placine 
them as Auto Electrician in Excavation Group-D with effect 
from 30-11-83 and now there is no dispute regarding the 
regularisation of the concerned workmen to Excavation Group- 
D. The management, however, has to pay the difference of 
wages of Group-E and D to the concerned workmen from 
30-1-83 to 10-11-86 us the management has already put a 
restriction for the payment of the difference of wages for the 
said period. The management is directed to pay the difference 
of wages of Excavation Gronp-E and Excavation Group-D to 
the concerned workmen from 30-11-83 to 10-11-86 within 
one month from the date of publication of the Award. 

This Is my Avvard.

In the matter of an industrial dispute under Section lOflKdl 
of the 1. D. Act, i947

Employers in relation to the management of P.R.S.P.

and their workmen,
